Thus Spoke the Hero Back from Another World
===========================================

0-01 "Prologue / or, the Epilogue of a Story Already Ended" — Part 1
-------------------

Looking at the midterm results handed back to him, he let out a quiet breath of relief.

His friend in the seat in front must have caught it, because he made a small "oh" and turned around with a smile.

"Sounds like you did pretty well this time."

"Yeah."

He answered with a smile of his own.

"I'm still nowhere near the top of the class, but compared to a little while ago my grades have recovered a lot. It's thanks to you. Thanks."

At the frank gratitude, his friend waved a hand as if embarrassed.

"Don't mention it. Seriously, you're embarrassing me."

"Am I? Well — they're certainly not good enough to get complacent about... but just clawing my way back this far was hard. I'm going to thank you for studying with me."

"Eh, it goes both ways. If I'm keeping you company while you're working seriously, then I don't slack off either."

The closing homeroom was over, and their classmates were drifting off in twos and threes, heading home or heading for club activities. His friend watched them go, speaking offhandedly.

He, too, was packing his things into his school bag as he looked down at the schoolyard through the window.

Below, the more eager members of the sports clubs had already started their warm-ups.

Off to one side were students walking out of the building in little groups — going off to have fun somewhere, most likely.

A thoroughly peaceful scene, the everyday landscape of a high-school student in modern Japan.

He watched that unremarkable sight through narrowed eyes.

"...What's up? You're spacing out."

His friend tilted his head and asked.

Nothing, he said, shaking his head at once, and changed the subject.

"Never mind that. About thanking you for the studying—"

"No, like I said, you don't have to. ...Ah, well, in that case—"

His friend glanced out the door toward the corridor.

Then he brought his eyes back and said, laughing teasingly:

"Introduce me to an underclassman girl sometime. Turns out I'm into younger girls."

"Turns out, huh... no, don't ask me for something like that. You know why, right?"

"I'm not asking *you*. Look, your escort's here. Makes a guy jealous, honestly."

"Mm—"

Sure enough, at the far end of the corridor, near the classroom door, a girl was waving at him.

"Ah, you finally looked my way. Let's head home, Daiki-senpai."

"...Right."

At his quiet answer, his friend — watching Daiki — shrugged.

"You two really do get along, though."

"...Does it look that way?"

"I mean, obviously. I don't know any other brother and sister that close. What's the deal — is she secretly a stepsister you're not actually related to?"

He fell silent, his expression complicated, and his friend laughed lightly and stretched.

"I'm kidding. Too much manga, that's all. Right, guess I'd better get to club..."

"Huh, is today a manga club day?"

"Nope, but I've got something to take care of. Meeting about my next piece."

"You've got an idea?"

"Who knows... Maybe I'll try an other-world story for once. Summoned to another world, becomes a hero, kills the Demon Lord, that sort of thing. Swinging a cool holy sword around."

"...Ha ha. Then I'll head home too. See you."

That said, he shoved the report card with «Daiki Kurosu» written on it into his bag and stood.

Out in the corridor, the girl from before fell quietly into step beside him, close enough to be leaning in.

"How were your midterm results?"

"Mm, well... finally about average, I'd say."

"That's wonderful. All that studying paid off, Daiki-senpai."

"Uh. Thanks, I guess."

"...Aren't you going to ask how Nagi did?"

The girl peered up at him. Daiki thought a moment before answering.

"No, I figured there was nothing to worry about where you're concerned. Your grades are always near the top, aren't they?"

"Hmm. Well then, let's say that's what it was. I'll let it pass, for senpai's sake."

The girl — Nagi Kurosu — smiled faintly.

After thinking about it a little, Daiki asked her:

"...I've been wondering for a while. Why do you call me «senpai»?"

"Is it strange?"

"When you ask it like that, I'm stuck for an answer. But at home it's «nii-san», isn't it?"

"Hee hee. Well, what's the harm — at school, at least? A little variety ought to keep senpai from getting bored of it. Call it a younger sister's consideration."

"...I don't think anyone gets bored of what they're called."

Well, if she was happy with it, maybe that was fine. Daiki left it at that.

Along the corridor, down the stairs, and out to the entrance hall.

He had nothing in particular planned afterward.

Reviewing the returned midterms wasn't urgent.

Nagi probably intended to walk home with him just like this. That was fine too, he thought — and then, right there at the entrance hall, he happened to spot a familiar figure taking her shoes out of the lockers.

"Ah..."

"—!"

She seemed to have noticed him as well.

Their eyes met for an instant, and hers were immediately averted. The girl who was supposed to have been his childhood friend hurried out of the building, all but fleeing from him.

"She's as rude as ever."

The words carried a barb, which was unusual for the soft-mannered Nagi.

Then again, after a display like that, perhaps it wasn't unreasonable.

Daiki shook his head lightly and said,

"Sorry, Nagi. There's somewhere I want to stop by, so would you go on ahead?"

"...Daiki-senpai."

"It's not like I'm going after her. She can't stand me; I'm not about to go out of my way to get close. I just want to detour somewhere, clear my head, and get the midterm review out of the way while I'm at it."

"In that case—"

"And I thought I'd stop at a bookshop too. So — that sort of thing."

"...Understood. Then I'll be going on ahead — **nii-san**."

Being called «nii-san» in that stiff tone was presumably some kind of statement on her part.

He might have done something unkind. But he genuinely did want a change of air, and — though he certainly couldn't say it out loud — having Nagi there unsettled him.

Even if he had to force it, he wanted to be alone.

"...A little sister, huh..."

Seeing Nagi off and then leaving the school building himself, Daiki murmured it under his breath.

He couldn't gauge the distance. Not with Nagi, his sister, and not with the childhood friend he was supposed to have been close to.

No — it wasn't only them.

It was his friend in the manga club, with whom he talked perfectly normally, and everything else besides.

—He understood the cause.

It was nothing other than the fact that he did not understand where he himself was standing.

Without even a clear sense of his own position, there was no way he could communicate properly with anyone other than himself.

"Where... am I, I wonder."

The murmur — in a way a thoroughly adolescent one, entirely appropriate to his age — dissolved into the clear pre-summer air.

Daiki narrowed his eyes against a refreshingly clear sky with few clouds, and sunlight at a pleasant temperature.

And yet.

Those words were a question he truly felt, from the heart.

Yes — a **question**.

Not a worry, not an inner conflict: a pure riddle, the kind that ought to have an answer.

Where is this place?

Where am I?

For about a year now, Daiki had been thinking about that without pause.

This world was nothing but wrongness, everywhere he looked.

"—**The thing is**, **I never had a little sister named Nagi**..."

　　　　　※

In the end he'd stayed until the place closed.

Pushed out by a staff member's "We'll be closing shortly," Daiki Kurosu was walking through town at ten in the evening — an hour that could fairly be called night.

"I concentrated too hard... What do I do? It'd be awkward to get picked up for curfew."

Socially speaking, Daiki's station was that of a second-year high-school student.

In uniform, needless to say. Strolling brazenly through the entertainment district was hardly advisable.

After a moment's thought, Daiki decided to take the back streets home. It would be a bit of a detour, but he didn't mind moving his body. He'd get off the main roads and make his way home that way.

Carrying his school bag, and a plastic bag with the two paperbacks he'd bought at the bookshop he'd visited before the café.

From any angle, he'd look like an ordinary male high-school student on his way home from school.

That was what everyday life was. An utterly commonplace, peaceful existence, the kind found anywhere.

Was such a thing sturdy? Or was it fragile?

Daiki, at least, was not in possession of the answer.

But even so.

"Ngh—?! What, is this... I feel... sick—?!"

—He understood immediately that he had walked straight into the moment when everyday life (*it*) was destroyed.

What struck Daiki then, out of nowhere, was an overwhelming revulsion.

It wasn't that anything was wrong with his body. There was no pain. Only a violent feeling of disgust, as though it had been poured directly into his brain — a sensation of being sick to his stomach with no way around it.

And with it an agitation that would not be satisfied until he cut the sensation off at its source, right now.

"...Tch."

And so Daiki's steps drifted, unsteadily, in a certain direction, without his even being aware of it.

Off the road home, toward somewhere emptier of people — no, toward **a space that no human should have been able to enter**.

That the place had been made **that way** was likewise beyond Daiki's awareness.

The feeling was close to drunkenness. As if strong spirits saturated the air and he had breathed them in. He turned into a narrow alley, and by degrees the surroundings opened out into a spot hemmed in by run-down buildings.

"...Huh. Why am I somewhere like this...?"

It was a place he would never have passed through on his way home. Not so much a detour as the wrong direction entirely.

He understood that this was **wrong**, and still his feet would not stop carrying him forward.

And so what came next was the natural conclusion.

Daiki turned the corner of the alley ahead of him.

Past the bend was a slightly wider dead end, with moonlight falling in from above.

But what stole his eyes more than anything was the too-vivid red that coloured the place.

The whole surface was stained with red in great quantity, as though a can of paint had been overturned, and it ran everywhere across the ground in geometric figures, following some fixed set of rules.

More abnormal still was that the red patterns drawn on the ground were giving off a faint light. The reason he could make them out clearly even in the gloom was the red glow welling up from underfoot rather than the moonlight.

The smell of iron stung his nose.

Daiki understood perfectly well that it was **a magic circle drawn in blood**.

"...This is bad, isn't it."

The instant he murmured it, the faint light being cast drained away rapidly, and went to zero.

Like this, the atmosphere was exactly that of a grisly murder scene. And in fact, if this much blood had come from a single person, there was no way that person was still alive.

Fortunately, though — if that was the right word — he could find nothing resembling a corpse in the vicinity.

All that was here now was a magic circle drawn in blood. That was all.

"What am I... supposed to do about this? Should I call the police or something...?"

Or would it be wiser to pretend he hadn't seen it and turn on his heel?

The briefest hesitation.

What proved fatal, though, was not that instant, but the fact of having come to this place at all.

If it was a matter of being too late, then by this point there was nothing Daiki could have done.

An instant later, Daiki sprang lightly, the smallest of steps, forward.

Twisting his body, turning as he leapt.

Something was being swung through the spot he had occupied a heartbeat earlier — a fact he confirmed by sensation rather than by sight.

—**He had been attacked**.

Two people were surprised there.

If the two were measured against each other, the greater surprise surely belonged not to Daiki, who had realised he was being attacked from behind, but to the assailant whose surprise knifehand had been dodged so easily.

"That was close..."

Daiki murmured it quietly.

It probably wasn't a reply, but the other person present spoke, with a note of understanding.

"Dodged it, huh... Well, of course you would, standing in a place like this. I was hedging my bets, and I suppose that's what you'd call softness on my part. Right — I'll chalk it up to tuition."

He heard a tap, tap — the sound of a toe striking the ground.

Keeping his distance, narrowing his eyes, Daiki caught the source of the sound and the words at the far end of his gaze.

—It was a girl.

About his own age, perhaps. Dressed all in black — some school's uniform? — she wore it and melted into the darkness.

Her short hair was black as well; the only feature that stood out in the night was her crimson eyes.

They lowered slightly, and with a sigh the girl went on, sounding as if it were a chore.

"I don't want to drag this out, so I'll say only what needs saying: if you're going to surrender, do it now. I'm here as the deputy of this city's administrator, more or less. You know what that means. I'm no champion of justice either — do it somewhere else and I won't come after you — but if you're going to pull something in this place, I can't afford to be lenient."

"..."

Vaguely, at least.

—Wait, am I being taken for the culprit who painted this place in blood?

That was what Daiki gathered.

He'd been sure the one who attacked him was the culprit and that he was about to be silenced; but if that was the situation, he might be able to clear up the misunderstanding.

Though even so, the fact that a girl like this was chasing the culprit struck him as strange.

"Um. I think there might be some kind of misunderstanding here..."

At any rate, Daiki opened his mouth to talk it out.

To her, however, the remark apparently rang thoroughly false.

The girl's crimson eyes pierced him sharply.

"—Are you making fun of me?"

"Huh? No, I didn't mean—"

"No, it's fine. I'm used to it, so that part's fine. But I'd rather not be taken for someone you can fob off with words like that. Fine. Understood. —I've delivered my warning properly, at least."

"Wh-whoa... I get the feeling this conversation is rolling in a bad direction."

At the thoroughly unwelcome premonition, the corner of Daiki's mouth twitched.

The girl, meanwhile, sank slightly and took a guard with no opening in it. Plainly a battle stance.

—Oh, for heaven's sake. No help for it.

Daiki steeled himself.

When sparks come falling on you, you have to run at full speed or you'll get burned.

Honestly, he had almost no grasp of what was going on, but the one future that was perfectly clear was that at this rate he was going to be attacked.

Her standing on the side with the way out was a nuisance, but he was confident he could manage something.

And then—

"—Hah!"

The girl burst forward.

0-02 "Prologue / or, the Epilogue of a Story Already Ended" — Part 2
-------------------

Kicking hard off the ground, she came in low and drove straight inside his guard in one motion.

Sinking from darkness into darkness, the movement was hard to follow.

—Not going to react in time—!

All Daiki could do was cross his arms and block the blow.

Fortunately, comparing builds, even Daiki — who was hardly a big man — far outmatched the girl.

The guard should hold.

That such optimism did not apply was a lesson Daiki was made to learn with his own body.

"Rrraaah!"

"Gkh—"

From a posture that was practically a crouch, the fist came up along an impossible, springing trajectory.

The girl's right fist buried itself into the middle of his crossed left arm, and Daiki felt a crunch of breaking bone from inside his own body — the blow was strong enough to make him think so, at least.

"Ghk, gh, gaah—?!"

His body was flung back and he rolled across the concrete. It left scrapes here and there, but the impact was such that he couldn't spare attention for anything so minor.

The force was impossible. For a moment he genuinely thought he'd been shot through from the ground by a pile driver (*pile bunker*).

Daiki somehow got his posture back and pushed himself up onto one knee, but the absurd strength of the pain from his left arm wouldn't permit him anything more.

"Ow, ow... ugh, damn it, seriously...!"

"...Hmm?"

The girl, meanwhile, made a sound like she was questioning something.

He wanted to say that *he* was the one who ought to be tilting his head, but of course he couldn't.

"You jumped back and killed the force...? No, but— actually, before that—"

—What is this person talking about?

Daiki genuinely wondered. He'd rather she didn't say it so casually.

Granted, he'd tried to do something like that.

Even so, it hadn't gone as neatly as in a manga or an anime; his left arm was very likely just plain broken, and rolling across the ground had hurt too.

It was more that he'd merely avoided having his organs rupture and his whole body burst from a single hit.

The girl, though, apparently wasn't questioning things for that reason alone.

"Actually— huh, wait. That uniform... huh, you're a student?"

At this point the girl widened her eyes as if only now recognising what Daiki looked like.

"...Is she seriously telling me she hadn't noticed until this very second...?"

It had been dark, true. But even so.

Wrapped in a sense of dissatisfaction, plus the searing pain in his left arm, Daiki watched the situation unfold.

And then, right in front of him, the girl said:

"—Um. Who are you?"

"Am I allowed to take that as an admission that you decked someone by mistake?"

The girl pressed her lips into a flat line and made an entertaining face, breaking out in a cold sweat.

"No. No, no, no, there's no way. There's no way an ordinary person is in a place like this. I checked properly at the start, too. —Ah, th-that's it! The way you moved is impossible!"

"..."

"Nngh, no, but the age really is too far off, and the report said he wasn't this much of a fighter, which is exactly why the job came to me in the first place... no, but, huh...? Then who are you?!"

For just an instant, he considered asking who *she* was in return.

He gave up on that and answered honestly instead. His arm hurt; he wasn't in the mood for games.

"...Daiki Kurosu. High-school student."

Having said that much, he considered whether something more was called for.

And Daiki appended these words to his self-introduction:

"—A perfectly ordinary one."

"Like hell a perfectly ordinary high-school student moves like that!"

The girl clutched her head and howled it.

...Well, fair enough.

Daiki thought so too. Even if he'd had no other options, he had gone and done something an average high-school student couldn't. Which seemed to have got him strangely suspected.

Still, if one spoke strictly of the present, there wasn't a single lie in what he'd just said.

"...For now, is it fine to say the misunderstanding's cleared up? I'd appreciate an explanation of why I got jumped out of nowhere, if possible."

Daiki stood as he asked. But the girl still seemed to be in a state of confusion.

"Huh, wait. No, seriously, wait. I still can't accept this!"

"...Which part?"

"Which part... Okay, I accept that you're not the target I'm chasing. But being told you're a perfectly ordinary high-school student — that part I can't accept."

Settling her confusion, the girl spoke.

The misunderstanding aside, her wariness had not yet been lowered.

"What are you? I'd like a detailed explanation from your side too."

"...Even if you say that."

"I know I'm being unreasonable. If I find out you're unrelated, I'll take whatever responsibility afterward. But as long as I'm here on a job, I can't just leave a suspicious character alone."

"Ah... no, that's not what I meant."

Explaining his circumstances was difficult.

Well — saying it was easy enough.

But he couldn't imagine, not by any stretch, that he'd be believed.

What to do, Daiki wondered.

He'd demanded an explanation a moment ago, but it was all starting to feel like a hassle, and his arm hurt, and honestly he'd rather just be let go. Or be looked after, that would do too.

The girl watched him waver, let out a breath, and then said:

"All right. I'm going to contact an acquaintance for a second, and then—"

It happened in that instant.

—All at once, the magic circle of blood began to glow again.

"Magical energy?! Why— it *is* you after all!"

"It's not, it wasn't me! And what did you just say? Magical energy?!"

The red light gathered strength.

Unlike before, it brightened in an instant — and in an instant went out again.

Neither Daiki nor, it seemed, even the girl understood everything about what was happening and why.

With an expression of complete incomprehension, the girl threw a glance at Daiki.

"It reacted...? To me? Or else... Hey, you—"

"—Look out!"

"Eek?!"

It was reflexive. This time Daiki was the one shouting, and he dived at the girl.

Caught off guard, the girl was borne down onto the ground under him.

"What are you—"

"Don't struggle, my arm hurts! And get up, now!"

With the situation changing by the second, the confusion was mutual.

Even so, Daiki was the slightly calmer of the two.

He got off the girl before he could be misunderstood again, and turned his eyes to the front.

The girl saw it as well.

Standing at the end of his gaze: a black, four-legged creature with an enormous body.

"Wha..."

No — whether it was accurate to call that thing a creature, the girl at least could not say with confidence.

That black bulk was far too inorganic to be called a beast, and nonetheless it held murderous intent.

"...Ngh..."

Groaning, Daiki pressed a hand to his arm. He'd overexerted it and made it worse.

Because he'd shielded her — and once she understood that, what the girl would do was decided.

"...Thanks. Stay back, I'll handle it."

She was done thinking about what he really was.

In this situation, he had protected her, a total stranger.

That debt had to be repaid.

"Much obliged... though it feels like a weird thing to be saying."

"Well, I'm the one who broke your arm... I said I was sorry."

"Never mind that — can you beat it? That thing."

"Who knows? I've never seen anything like it... a familiar or something of that sort, I'd assume. Though for that, its presence feels off somehow."

"...No," Daiki said.

"**That's a monster**."

"...What are you talking about?"

"Hard to explain. Anyway, just think of it as the type of creature that pours its heart and soul into killing humans. That's roughly correct."

"What kind of worst-possible creature is that. ...So, does it have a weakness?"

The girl decided not to think about the details.

There were countless questions.

Naturally so, when a mere high-school student who called himself perfectly ordinary was speaking about something even she had never seen as though it were familiar.

But it was fine.

Because for now, for this moment alone, the girl had decided to trust him.

"It has a core. Around the crown of its head, probably."

"A core..."

"You can think of it as something like a stone. Crush that and it goes down."

"I see... yes, if I focus and look, I can make it out."

"I can't see it myself, but... well, if you can, that's fine. Conversely, attacking anywhere else is almost meaningless, so keep that in mind. ...Can you do it?"

"Easily."

As she spoke, the girl turned side-on to the black beast. She extended an arm in front of her, palm up, index finger pointed — the posture of someone taking aim with a ranged weapon, if one had to compare it to something.

Conversely, it was a stance that would be hard to recover from if the thing pounced.

Daiki couldn't read her intent.

Even so — Daiki said nothing.

And then.

"—«Thorn»—"

Just those words, and nothing else.

With the word as a trigger, something black was loosed from the girl's fingertip — or so it looked to Daiki.

Strictly speaking he hadn't been able to see it. It was dark here, and above all the movement had been too fast.

But as a result, whatever the girl had loosed from her fingertip had unmistakably pierced the monster through the crown of its head. Its core crushed, unable to maintain its existence, the monster dispersed into the air like mist.

"...What was that just now..."

Daiki's words were less a question than a stunned murmur; the girl shrugged and answered lightly.

"Just what it looked like. My magecraft."

"...Your what?"

"Is that so surprising? You're throwing around words like 'monster,' so it can't be your first time seeing a magus. And come to that, aren't you a magus too?"

"No, well... not really, on both counts."

"...What is that supposed to mean. I really don't understand this person at all... Ahem!"

The girl cleared her throat with pointed deliberateness, then slowly approached Daiki.

Standing in front of him, she extended a hand toward him. And at the same time she said:

"Let's start over. I'm Narumi. —Narumi Oki. A magus of no importance. And you're, um... Daiki, was it?"

"Mm, yeah. Daiki Kurosu."

"Then — nice to meet you. Not that it'll be a long acquaintance."

"...Likewise, I guess?"

Thinking that this had turned into something strange, Daiki answered the handshake.

The girl — Oki — smiled thinly as she took his hand, and pulled it hard toward her.

"Whoa—"

Her face closed in all at once.

Seen properly, Oki was a girl with well-arranged features.

"So? I'd be glad if Daiki's side would reveal his true identity around now too."

Crimson eyes fixed straight through him. Pressed by them, Daiki spoke.

"Ah, no... it's not that I have a true identity or anything; the perfectly ordinary high-school student part isn't a lie. It's just—"

"Just?"

"—**The thing is**, **a while back**, **I got sent to another world**."

"......Huh?"

"I got a holy sword there, killed the Demon Lord... well, travelled for about five years. And then last year I came back to Earth, sort of? That's about the shape of it, anyway..."

"..."

"No, um... er. Narumi-san?"

"Oki is fine, Daiki. Hm?"

"Is it. No, um, that's not the issue. Oki. Oki-san, the... my hand hurts."

Oki was showing him a sweet, lovely smile, right in front of his face.

So she could make an expression like that too, Daiki found himself vaguely impressed — except this was no time for that.

"Um. Ow. The handshake hurts, it really hurts, you're squeezing way too hard, ow. Seriously strong, strong, strong, ow."

"Hey, Daiki. That just now — would that be you making fun of me, I wonder?"

"That's really not my intention. No, I'm honestly being completely serious, I'm sorry."

"Then did you hit your head or something?"

"My left arm got punched through, but— I mean ow, ow, it really hurts, you're going to crush my right hand too!"

"—There's no way I can believe that!"

"That's exactly why I didn't want to say it... And magi are about the same thing, aren't they? There's no way people like that exist as a matter of course — think about it with common sense."

"C-common sense?! I'm being lectured on common sense by a guy who just announced he went to another world?!"

—But, unfortunately.

Just as, for instance, **Narumi Oki was in fact a magus**,

**what Daiki Kurosu said was fact**, **and he had once truly been the hero who saved another world**.

He had carried the holy sword, been called a Hero, and struck down the Demon Lord with overwhelming power.

And then, returning to Earth, he lost every scrap of that power and went back to being nothing more than **a perfectly ordinary high-school student who was just a bit used to the last-ditch moment**.

And therefore, he does not know.

The fact that this Earth conceals an extraordinary to rival even another world.

That magi, or the Empowered, monsters, inhuman things, supernatural existences out of science fiction and fantasy, genuinely do exist — Daiki Kurosu has no way of knowing it.

So.

Even if the everyday life he clawed back should be threatened by such supernatural beings.

He has no strength left with which to fight.

Nor can he acquire any.

There is no one who knows that he was a hero, no way to prove it, and the boy is not permitted to resist what is unreasonable.

And in fact, he has been dragged in.

That he is unhelpfully calm, that he can, after a fashion, cope with the situation — these facts are nothing but a curse-like after-effect luring him toward an extraordinary he does not want.

Though he truly is an ordinary person with no power whatsoever, he ends up suspected of being an extraordinary man harbouring some secret.

But. Even so.

—**Daiki Kurosu is nothing more than a perfectly ordinary high-school student**.

1-01 "The Former Hero, and the Witch" — 1
-------------------

If asked whether there had been any warning signs, Daiki would have to answer that there had been none.

He went to sleep, woke up, and before he knew it he was in another world.

It had been so abrupt that there was no other way to describe it: before he knew it Daiki had been transferred to an other world he had never seen or heard of, and there was no option but to live through it — put into words, that was all there was to it.

He had been a first-year high-school student at the time.

It had been a brutal road.

Battles in which it would not have been strange to die at any moment.

Even so, together with a holy sword that possessed a will and companions he could rely on, Daiki had adventured the length and breadth of the world — of the other world — and at last succeeded in his goal, the slaying of the Demon Lord.

And then, with the same abruptness as when he had arrived, he came to himself back here on Earth.

—And with no passage of time whatsoever.

　　　　　※

"Um. Hold on a second, let me get this straight."

—Afterward.

Saying she would "clear up," Oki had wiped away what remained on the spot — the thing that appeared to be a blood magic circle — until it was clean. And in almost an instant, at that.

Some sort of magic — or rather, «magecraft», as Oki called it — must have been employed.

Daiki was well used to the sight of magic itself from the other world, but seeing it on Earth was a first. The system itself seemed to be different; what had been done, Daiki could not begin to understand.

Once the disposal was finished, Oki went on to treat Daiki's arm.

Though as she put it: "It's strictly first aid, all right? I did use healing magecraft, technically, but frankly at my level of skill it's a drop of water on a hot stone. So this is about all I can do. ...Sorry."

Instead, she first touched his arm, and then with some mysterious pitch-black thread-like thing produced from that hand (Daiki had no idea whatsoever what it was) she wound his left arm around and around.

The black thread had no thickness to it — no mass, rather — that he could feel; the sensation was that of a shadow, if one had to compare.

"...It's gone pitch black."

Daiki looked at the thread wound directly against his skin as he said it.

Though by now it was less thread than cloth or tape. It looked as if it had melted together into a single piece — or rather, all that was visible was a jet black that seemed to let no light through at all.

"Call it reinforcement. I anaesthetised it with magical energy, so you shouldn't feel any pain either."

"...You're right, I really don't."

Daiki swung his arm lightly to check. Oki pushed the hand down with a "Hey, now."

"It isn't healed, so keep still. You'll be fine as long as you do, but I think you should have an ordinary doctor look at it later."

"Got it. Well, either way you saved me. Thanks, Oki."

"Uu, ah... Um, er... I'm sorry. For, um, injuring you... that is."

Rather than pleasure, Daiki's smiling thanks seemed to prick Oki's conscience, and she offered a meek apology. Now, belatedly, she seemed to deeply regret having attacked him.

Which was precisely why Daiki laughed and forgave her. If it was a misunderstanding, it couldn't be helped. He wasn't going to blame her past that.

—After that, Daiki did as she told him and set off walking with Oki.

He sent a text to his family to let them know he'd be home late, just in case. Nagi might worry.

Once they'd started walking, Oki asked again about Daiki's circumstances.

Beside Daiki — who omitted nearly all the details and conveyed only the essentials — Oki put both hands to her temples and made a small "mrrgh."

"So what you're saying is: you went to sleep, woke up in another world, spent five whole years travelling, and then the moment you thought you'd achieved your goal you were back — but on Earth not even a day had passed?"

"Whether it was exactly five years, I don't know. The calendar over there was different from this one. It was about that long, roughly. And I don't actually know whether the timing on this side lined up exactly either... but at the least, nobody was aware of me ever having gone missing."

"Mm... well, I suppose the fine details can wait for now."

Oki looked a little reluctant to say it, but she seemed at least to be granting that Daiki wasn't lying.

Even that much, Daiki thought, was more than enough to be thankful for.

"I've wondered myself whether I was dreaming. Hallucinations, delusions, that kind of thing — I can't honestly claim I've never once suspected it."

"I-I wouldn't go quite that far myself..."

Oki looked somehow concerned on his behalf, and Daiki couldn't help a wry smile.

She seemed to be a conscientious sort of girl. Daiki wouldn't have found it strange to be laughed off out of hand.

"But at the very least, I definitely have memories of my experiences in the other world. Monsters, demonkind, and ordinary humans too. Memories of having fought them — those are definitely still with me."

In that sense, Daiki's mental age worked out to something like twenty.

Rich in combat experience in another world, called nothing less than a hero — and now a perfectly ordinary high-school student.

"...Well, after what I saw earlier, I don't take you for an amateur either."

"Unfortunately my physical ability dropped off a cliff. Over there I had more — how to put it — manga-grade strength. I wasn't a mage, but I could manage an imitation of it. There's a thing called the holy sword's blessing, and anyway, I was stronger than a normal human."

"A H-Hero..."

"Hey, cut that out. Being called that makes me want to crawl into a hole. I haven't told anyone but you about this, you know. Keep it secret — I don't want people thinking I'm one of those embarrassing types."

"Huh, ah, right. Is that so. I see... I-I'm the first...! H-hmmm?"

—...Why is she getting flustered?

Daiki wondered, but before he could say anything Oki shook her head.

"N-no, well, honestly I nearly thought the same thing... but it cuts both ways, I figured."

"...You're a magus, apparently. Honestly, from where I stand that's the stranger part. This is Earth."

"It's a bit much when you say it like it'd be acceptable in another world."

"In another world it would be acceptable."

"It's the other world that isn't."

"When you put it like that, the argument's over..."

In fact, from the standpoint of someone who had actually been to another world, it was as if he had come back from a fantasy world to ordinary life only to be told that Earth was a perfectly adequate fantasy as well.

Perhaps it was «mutual», as Oki said, but even so, from Daiki's point of view an Earth-grown magus was harder to believe in than the existence of another world.

Give him a break.

Naturally, that was purely Daiki's perspective; from Oki's side it looked different.

"There are tons of magi. Far more of us than people who've been to another world, at any rate."

"I-is that so...? Tons? There are tons?"

"Yes, tons. All over the world. You can take it that on the underside of the underside of society, there's a world of magi."

"I'd rather not take it... this is too much culture shock. I didn't want to get it on Earth as well."

"Says the man from another world."

"I'm not from another world, all right? And what, even magi don't know other worlds exist?"

"Mmm... at least it's never been publicly proven. Other worlds are a different matter from parallel-world theory. That one, at least, is supposed to have been proven magically."

"...............Parallel worlds."

"Yes, the 'parallel world' thing. Not that it's particularly relevant, but... something the matter?"

"Ah... no. It's nothing."

He shook his head to drive off the unamusing notion that had surfaced in his mind.

Then Daiki asked Oki a question, as if changing the subject.

"But if there really are that many of these magi, don't people just find out? I mean, look, I met you like this."

"H-hang on, *met*—?! D-don't say embarrassing things like that!!"

"...Huh? Which part?"

"Hyeh?! Uah... ah, no, it's nothing, never mind...! Ahem!"

Apparently Oki had a habit of clearing her throat theatrically when she wanted to change the subject.

Daiki thought so, but didn't put it into words.

"Normally an ordinary person absolutely doesn't get dragged in like this. There was a barrier over that place, too. Which is exactly why I assumed that anyone standing inside it — you — was involved."

"A barrier, huh... they had those in the other world too."

"Oh, they did?"

"Yeah. Defensive barriers, to block enemy tactical-class magecraft and the like. I could use them through the holy sword's functions."

"...I just heard a terrifying term dropped like it was nothing, but I'll let it slide..."

Oki's expression twitched slightly.

Daiki himself thought: what anime's setting is this, exactly.

"But that means barriers on this side are different?"

"Mmm... if what you're imagining is the shield or barrier sort from games, then it's different from what I meant. A barrier in magecraft over here is, simply put, mostly a thing that obstructs intrusion into the inside. 'One who is a magus shall remain unidentified' — that sort of principle."

"Huh... that's kind of interesting."

"Is it? Well, if you're impressed, fine. Anyway, that place had one as well, with a consciousness interference that makes ordinary people unconsciously avoid going near. Magical energy is fundamentally that kind of substance. Normal humans fear «the arcane» below the level of awareness, and shun it."

"..."

"But you walked right inside like it was nothing. —Why were you in that place?"

The reason he had gone there.

A place he ought to have avoided unconsciously — Daiki had, on the contrary, been unconsciously drawn to it.

"I suddenly started feeling sick, somehow. And then, yeah... it felt like I had to get rid of whatever was making me feel sick. After that it was unconscious; before I knew it I was heading there."

"......I see."

"Did you figure something out?"

"No, sorry. Not a thing. It makes no sense — but you did go to another world, so if there's a cause it'll be connected to that, obviously. That's about all I can say right now."

"......I see."

There was nothing else he could say. That was all he had learned. —Which was to say, nothing.

Still, it seemed that as a result of coming back from another world he had lost his power but might have been left with a troublesome after-effect.

Knowing even that much was progress, Daiki decided to think, optimistically.

"Even so, you really did believe me."

Daiki said it. As things stood, Oki was proceeding on the premise that he had come back from another world.

He was grateful, but if asked whether he would have believed it in her position, Daiki himself had no confidence.

"Well, you looked at that creature and said it was a monster — you did, didn't you."

Oki said it lightly.

To Daiki, whose eyes widened, she continued:

"You knew the weak point of something even I had never seen. Which means you have at least one piece of knowledge I don't. ...That thing — in other words, **you'd seen it in the other world**, hadn't you?"

"...Yeah."

Daiki nodded deeply.

And at the same time, Daiki was certain of it: that journey in the other world had not been a dream.

"That was a creature from the world I wandered into. They're called monsters — things that exist in order to kill humans. I fought them and killed them more times than I can count."

"Wow, so cool."

"Sorry, stop, I'm sorry, that's not it, I wasn't trying to sound cool—"

"Ahaha, joking, joking. No, but seriously, that's actually a pretty big deal..."

Oki brought a hand up in front of her mouth as though thinking.

Then she turned to face Daiki — and showed him **black eyes**.

"...Huh? Oki, your eye colour, compared to a moment ago—"

"Say, Daiki. I've got a proposition for you — do you have a moment?"

Daiki's half-finished words were cut off by Oki's thin smile.

The girl wore a bewitching — an arcane — smile.

A lustrous smile that seemed likely to draw a person in.

And then her eyes **stained deep crimson once again**.

As though dyed by blood.

Or as though dyeing with blood — far too arcane a thing.

"...Oki?"

Daiki suddenly remembered.

If magi existed in this world.

If they were those who had lurked in the shadow of history since ancient times, moving in the dark.

Then in this world, what does one call a female magus?

The answer is fixed.

"—Why don't you make a contract with me?"

Humanity calls them witches.

1-02 "The Former Hero, and the Witch" — 2
-------------------

Without realising it, Daiki had swallowed hard.

The atmosphere was different.

The approachable friendliness of a moment before had withdrawn into shadow; from the girl standing in front of him came an air of something cut off — yes, if one had to name it, a «distance».

"I don't think it's a bad offer."

Narumi Oki said it with crimson eyes. Even her manner of speech had shifted slightly.

Daiki had seen her eyes go black for just an instant, and that was probably their original shade. They had been few in number, but people who possessed that kind of **special eye** — the sort termed a «Mystic Eye» — had existed in the other world Daiki was sent to as well.

As a rule they manifested some abnormal, special ability.

Daiki could imagine that Oki's eyes were plausibly of that kind.

But — more than that.

He simply thought that Oki's crimson eyes were beautiful.

"...Daiki?"

Realising he'd been staring, and joking to himself that maybe *that* was the Mystic Eye's effect, Daiki shook his head and answered her.

"Ah, sorry. I was staring a bit."

"...?"

"Ah, no, it's just — your eyes really stand out, Oki."

He'd meant it in the sense of catching the eye, but put that way it might be taken wrongly.

Daiki piled on more words, intending to shore it up.

"You know, um, they're pretty. Like jewels, or something. Red's good. I think it's good."

"......Excuse me?"

Oki tilted her head and narrowed her eyes, looking exasperated.

He'd changed the subject far too abruptly.

As Daiki panicked, thinking he might have put her off, Oki went on:

"What, all of a sudden. Pretty — it is nothing of the sort, thank you. No, honestly, if you think saying things like that will get me in a good mood, you are very much mistaken. The contract has to be done properly, so..."

"................"

"Wh-what. Say something! Why are you looking straight at me...?"

"......No, I'm just glad you're pleased."

"I am not pleased, thank you. I was merely a little happy. Thank you very much!"

She was an honest girl. The kind who could say thank you.

Daiki was quite thoroughly won over.

"Oh, for— ... I thought bringing out the Mystic Eye would set the mood, and instead it backfires."

"*That* was the reason for the Mystic Eye?"

He rather wished he hadn't heard that.

Daiki's spirits dropped a notch.

"Ahem!" Oki cleared her throat.

"Not bad at all... Plying your wiles to get on the other party's good side before a contract — that's what comes of having been to another world, I suppose. Indeed."

"It's really not like that..."

Oki's eyes had already gone back to black. That was evidently the original after all.

And she really had, apparently, changed her eye colour purely for the sake of atmosphere.

"Judging by that, you seem to understand what it means to form a contract with a magus."

"No, I don't. I understand almost nothing. Please don't skip the explanations on your own."

"Oh, is that so... Ahem. Well then — what I'm proposing is that you cooperate with me until this incident is resolved. A contract in that sense."

"You keep skipping steps in this conversation... First: what is «this incident» you're talking about?"

He gathered it had something to do with the blood magic circle he'd seen earlier.

But even that he didn't know in detail. He'd simply happened to stumble into that place.

—The night wind along the road was cool.

Come to think of it, where were they walking to? He'd more or less been following Oki wherever she led, but Daiki hadn't heard their destination.

As for direction, they were heading toward the entertainment district Daiki had been in after school — toward the area near the big station, which meant back the way he'd come. He'd really rather not be picked up for curfew...

"—Put very simply: repelling a magus who has trespassed into this city. That's my job."

So Oki said. Come to think of it, she had mentioned being here on a job.

"Magi have their own communities, you know. I said there were tons of us earlier, but compared with people who aren't magi, the numbers might as well be zero. Modern magi mostly hold down jobs on the surface, so you can think of it as a mutual-aid organisation among magi for surviving this harsh capitalist society of ours. —Neighbourhood association also acceptable."

"That's so mundane it kills the romance..."

"Magi are ordinary Japanese citizens as well. We have a duty to pay taxes."

"Well, now that you mention it, maybe so."

"Did I get off topic... At any rate, this city has a team of magi I'm nominally attached to. This is that team's turf, so to speak. Trespass by an outside magus — and still more the unauthorised performance of a magical rite — isn't permitted. It has to be policed."

"...I see."

When the other party was a magus, there were presumably things that couldn't easily be left to ordinary police or courts.

To police it, magi living in that territory had to move themselves.

"So: right now we know that a magus has entered this city without permission and is, on top of that, conducting some kind of magical experiment — you saw those blood patterns too, didn't you."

"...Those were—"

"Human blood. Though maybe that goes without saying."

"So a magus is — killing people?"

Before he realised it, Daiki's hands had clenched hard.

The pain in his left arm, which had subsided, came dully back, and that was what made him aware of it.

—If that was the truth, then it certainly wasn't something he could leave alone.

But at the thought, Oki said easily:

"Huh? Ah, no. That's not it."

"It's — it isn't...? Well, so much the better if not."

"If that were going on it'd have been a major incident long ago. There are methods to make it look like a disappearance, and of course there are magi who'd resort to that if it came to it. But the cost-performance on that is terrible."

"...'Cost-performance'..."

"That's the caster's own blood. He's using his own blood as a medium to work some magecraft or other."

"His own? But it didn't look like one person's worth of blood—"

"Not necessarily. If you can manipulate blood you can spread it thin, and he might have boosted or generated blood with magecraft. He can probably do that much."

"..."

—Is anything permitted so long as you say the word 'magecraft'?

Daiki nearly let himself be exasperated, but he'd seen similar things in the other world himself.

Griping about it at this stage might be a little uncouth.

"Well, I don't know what he's after, but I think he's at the experimental stage right now. So far he seems to have theory only, with no success. That's how high the difficulty of the rite he's performing is. And, well, you can probably guess — difficult means, correspondingly, that the effect is likely to be large too."

"So it's a pretty bad situation."

"Well, the biggest problem is that **the place he chose was here**; the content of it, frankly, is neither here nor there. I'm stopping him either way. So that's the story. He's the one I'm looking for."

"...I follow the story."

Daiki nodded slightly — but then shook his head.

"But from what you've said, I can't picture what I'd be able to help with. Supposing we make this contract, what is it you want from me?"

"You said it yourself a minute ago, didn't you? —**You felt sick**, **and before you knew it you were in that place**."

"...I did say that."

"You might be under a misapprehension because I showed up afterward, but this culprit is abnormally good at concealing himself. I think he has a talent for it. In fact, he was long gone by the time we were there, wasn't he? As things stand I'm always a step behind. But you got there faster than I did."

"By chance... I'd have thought."

"Chance or not, finding him sooner beats finding him later. Right? And besides, you're definitely safer with me. —Because you've probably already been marked."

"Huh?"

At Oki's words, which came out of nowhere, Daiki's eyes went round with surprise.

But Oki told him as though it were entirely obvious:

"I mean, it activated, didn't it, that magic circle. Naturally he detected it too."

"No, but..."

"Let me tell you, a failed magic circle starting up after the fact like that is something that absolutely does not happen normally. And you are unquestionably involved in it."

"Why... ah, no, I see. Then the reason the monster appeared there at that moment was...!"

Daiki got there, and Oki nodded.

"You got hurt back then, didn't you. —Not the arm, all right? When you rolled across the ground."

"Ah, yeah. Well, I got a few scrapes, is all."

"—Which, put another way, means **your blood adhered to the circle**."

Daiki knew practically nothing of the reasoning behind magecraft on Earth.

All he understood was that it differed fundamentally from the technical principles of the other world he had been in.

But even so, he grasped that Oki's words carried a heavy meaning.

"I still don't know the logic of it. I don't even know what that circle was for. But there's no doubt the circle reacted to you. In fact, a monster from another world appeared in that place. To put it more precisely — **something that on this Earth has a connection to no one but you**."

"..."

"And that's the reaction from a mere scrape. It wouldn't be at all strange for the caster to want a much larger quantity of your blood next time. ...No, that's not right. Your life is being targeted right now, for certain."

"My life..."

"I said the cost-performance was bad earlier, but inverted, that also means: **if the reward matches the killing, a magus won't shrink from murder**. So that's the contract."

Narumi Oki was looking straight into Daiki Kurosu's eyes.

Her black eyes wavered with unease, wet at the edges.

Even when they weren't red, he thought, they were beautiful.

"...It's my responsibility that you've been marked."

"No... I don't think that's true."

"**Then you had better think it.** Daiki, that kindness of yours isn't a virtue."

Those were sincere words, Daiki felt.

Forceful, but serious in tone. Without fearing the retort that she was one to talk, she was putting into words the thing that needed saying.

In that case, Daiki thought, he had to answer it in kind.

So he told her:

"**No.** **It still isn't your fault, Oki.**"

"Daiki...?"

"The one at fault is this magus, surely. Getting that backwards and blaming you accomplishes nothing. If anything, leaving a weird sore spot between us when we're about to work together would be stupid. Right?"

"Th-that... may be true, but. —I mean, 'about to work together'—"

"Let's do it. The contract. It's fine — believe it or not, I'm reasonably used to desperate straits. I've got experience serving as a Hero in another world, after all. Shall I write you a résumé?"

"...What is that."

Oki laughed a little then.

Not the taut expression from before, but a face suited to a girl her age.

"That's pretty cool."

"I'm a hero, after all. A former one."

At the joking exchange, the two looked at each other and laughed.

It was the first time he'd made a joke like that, Daiki thought.

He'd been dragged into a strange situation, and yet — why was it? For the first time in the past year, since coming back to this world, he felt at ease.

"Right! If that's decided, sooner is better. And we're just arriving anyway."

"Arriving... come to think of it, Oki, where are we headed?"

"Huh, did I not say? Well, given the hour, there's only one destination."

So saying, Oki pointed with one hand at a building ahead.

When Daiki followed her gesture with his eyes, what he found was a luxury apartment block.

"Top floor, up there. It's a corner unit, so the view's surprisingly good."

"...Um? So that means — where exactly is that, again?"

Wrapped in an odd sense of wrongness and premonition, Daiki asked fearfully.

And Oki, with an air of complete matter-of-factness, answered him:

"Huh? My room, obviously."

"...At this hour, wouldn't it trouble your family—"

"There's nobody there, so it's fine."

—No, that is not fine.

So Daiki thought, but at this point he couldn't very well say he was going home.

1-03 "The Former Hero, and the Witch" — 3
-------------------

Daiki's misgivings aside, strictly speaking the place was not Oki's home.

"Well, call it a guest house, or a hideout, or whatever. I'm allowed to use it freely for the duration of a job. Right now I'm borrowing the key. Extravagant, isn't it — I'm jealous..."

That was how Oki explained it.

In short, this was not a room that Oki (that the Narumi household) held any rights to.

Thinking about it calmly, of course she wasn't going to invite a man she'd just met to her family home.

Though if asked whether it was all right to invite him *here*, Daiki thought that was dubious too.

He decided not to think about it.

"Is this place owned by that team of magi, then?"

"More precisely, one of them provides it, I suppose. I said 'team,' but we're not organised strictly or anything. That part's a nuisance."

"Huh... do magi make good money?"

Being able to provide a room in what looked like a distinctly expensive apartment block for that purpose alone, they must be quite well off — so Daiki reasoned, roughly. A commoner's train of thought.

Oki answered it with a wry smile.

"Being a magus isn't an occupation."

"Huh? Ah... ah, I suppose that's true."

Daiki had a game-like image of it as a «job class», but now that she said it, it was certainly a delicate matter.

No method of monetising being a magus came immediately to mind.

Of course, if he thought about it there were surely plenty, but excluding anything that broke the law, and given that they apparently concealed their identities, nothing occurred to him on the spot.

The best he managed was the idiotic notion that they could become stage magicians.

"Magi are mostly poor."

Oki narrowed her eyes, gazing off at something distant as she said it.

"Well, there are exceptions, naturally. Old lineages and the like. And I hear there are plenty of wealthy ones abroad."

"...What about you, out of curiosity?"

"Magecraft might be a bit like a hobby that costs money... heh."

He gathered the gist. The world appeared to be a harsh place.

Daiki looked around the spacious living room. There was a large sofa and a wide, low table, and even a big flat-screen television on the wall. Like a model home.

"It really is a nice place."

"Right? Well, it helps, so I'm not complaining — now then."

Oki came to stand in front of Daiki, turned to face him, and composed a serious expression.

Daiki straightened up and waited for her words.

"—Let's begin the contract, shall we. First, sit there."

She indicated the sofa, and Daiki obediently sat down.

Oki settled neatly in beside him.

Seen in a bright place, she was quite slight.

"Er. So, I sign a contract or something?"

Was she unexpectedly rigorous about this sort of thing?

From the formality of it, Daiki had gathered she didn't intend to settle for a verbal promise — but it seemed there was more to it than that.

Oki smiled faintly. Her eyes were still black.

At moments like these — when she put on her face as a magus — Oki became oddly theatrical.

The usual casual air receded, and there was, indeed, a mystique fitting for a magus.

"A magus cannot be bound by the laws of men."

"...,"

"Well, strictly speaking, in daily life we're bound like everyone else."

"That was cooler before you said that. Even if I was thinking the same thing."

"...Ahem. Anyway. The only thing that binds a magus is the law that magi lay down for themselves."

So it seemed. Daiki could roughly grasp what she was driving at.

Magi presumably had rules that held good among magi. In the other world Daiki had been in, mages with real power had likewise required correspondingly particular handling.

"What we're forming is a contract of the soul. A law that absolutely must be observed, defined magically, binding on both sides. —Let me confirm once more. Am I to take it you're prepared for that?"

"...Yeah. No problem."

It was an answer Daiki had thought through and arrived at in his own way. He wouldn't regret it, he believed.

Whatever she supposed was going on inside him, Oki didn't press. She only nodded once, quietly.

"Then hold out your hand."

"Which one?"

"...The right will do."

When Daiki held out his right hand as told, Oki seized him by the wrist, pulled it toward her, and—

"Wh— wait, Oki—"

"Don't move, hold still. This will hurt a little."

An instant later, Oki set her teeth to Daiki's thumb and bit hard through the skin.

"—Ow, that—"

"Maybe the left would have been better. That one's anaesthetised with magical energy, so you wouldn't have felt it."

"...I'd have appreciated an explanation before you did that..."

"Don't be so spoiled with a magus. Mm, no, the right is better after all. A contract feels more real when it comes with pain. —Be aware that you're forming a contract with a magus, Daiki."

So saying, Oki went on to bite her own thumb as well.

Red fluid ran from her fingertip, somehow lascivious.

"You could just have let me do my own..."

Daiki said it. Oki tilted her head slightly.

"Could I? Ah, well, you'd probably have managed. People who aren't used to this sort of thing often can't bring themselves to do it, or make a mess of it. So, force of habit."

"Well, I'll take it as having been bitten by a pretty girl, then."

"..................What's that supposed to — is Daiki secretly a masochist, really, I'd rather you didn't say that kind of thing so casually to a girl, but thank you for the compliment — no, that's not the point!"

Was Oki perhaps unused to being complimented?

Watching the girl shout with her ears going red in front of him, Daiki found himself wondering.

"Your hand, come on. Out with it, your finger. Put it against mine."

Oki extended her thumb upright toward him. Daiki set his own against hers.

"Like this?"

"Yes. I mentioned something like this earlier, but blood is a strong medium for magecraft."

"I feel like I sort of understand that."

"That's enough. —All right, here we go."

The moment the words were out, Oki's eyes stained crimson.

Beneath a thin smile, the corner of her mouth twisting, the red-eyed magus spoke.

"I'll confirm the terms of the contract. I'll put it into words; you say one word of acceptance."

"Got it. Understood."

"—Thou, Daiki Kurosu. You shall, insofar as your own safety can be secured, cooperate with and lend your strength to the duties of I, Narumi Oki. And Narumi Oki shall, within that same limit, guarantee the safety of Daiki Kurosu, at the price of her own life if need be. —Okay?"

"Okay."

The instant he spoke his assent, he felt a strong heat at the fingertips where they touched.

At the same time, Daiki felt something like being drained, like strength draining out of him.

—And that too lasted an instant.

Oki's finger drew away at once, and Oki — whose eyes had returned to their colour at some point — smiled lightly and said:

"There, that's the end of it. Simple thing, isn't it?"

"...I guess. I thought there'd be more, I don't know, complicated patterns appearing."

"Ah... well, normally it does take a few more steps. In my case, thanks to these eyes, I can skip a fair bit of the procedure. Well, if it can be done efficiently, there's no problem."

Come to think of it, Daiki hadn't heard what effect Oki's Mystic Eye actually had. And from the explanation just now it was hard to get a clear grasp of it.

If he asked, would she tell him? While Daiki was considering trying it—

"—Nom."

Before he knew it, a soft sensation had touched his finger.

Oki had put her lips to the wound.

"Huh—"

"Mm... *lick*. Mm, okay. The bleeding's stopped."

"..."

"...What's wrong, Daiki? You've frozen up."

Daiki hesitated a good long while over what to say, or whether to let it pass, and then—

"No. ...That's kind of lewd."

—he said it.

Oki gazed up at him blankly for a while, but once she'd worked out what the words meant, her face went bright red and she waved both hands wildly.

"Wheh?! Ah, n-no, that's not—?! I-I do it that way normally, so, uwah, um!"

"I know. I was just teasing you a bit. —It did feel nice, though."

"Don't say things like thaaat!!"

Oki smacked Daiki's shoulder repeatedly.

Slight as she was, there was no power behind it. None of the arm strength that had sent him flying with one punch was in evidence.

"Honestly... You say mean things sometimes, Daiki. I think it's awful."

There was no air of a magus about the girl sulking with her lips pushed out.

Well, the more approachable version was more welcome to Daiki too.

Laughing lightly, Daiki said,

"You don't really feel like a magus, Oki."

"Huh...? That isn't a compliment, is it. Depending on who you say that to, it's a landmine."

"Ah, sorry. Though you certainly had the atmosphere when you first pressed me about the contract. How to put it... yeah, it felt like a witch—"

"—**Daiki**."

He'd said it without any thought behind it.

Oki seemed to set store by atmosphere, so if anything he'd meant the words as a bit of flattery to shore that up. Which is why —

he hadn't imagined that those few words alone would bring that atmosphere back to her.

"—**Do not**, **call me**, **a witch**."

A crimson glint bound his body up.

Her emotion had surged, and because she couldn't suppress it the red — a warning colour — had surfaced.

It wasn't that he'd forgotten.

But even so, he had to admit that he had been reminded.

That the person in front of him was by no means an ordinary girl.

—That she was a magus who, should she ever feel like it, could kill someone of his calibre with ease.

Daiki had no choice but to carve it into his chest.

"...Sorry. I didn't mean to offend you. If it's something I shouldn't say, I apologise."

At Daiki bowing his head honestly, Oki looked up with a start.

"—Ah, s-sorry! I'm the one who overreacted... I'm sorry, okay?"

"No. I don't really understand it, but that was an expression that upsets you. I'll be careful not to use it from now on, so it'd help if you let the first offence slide."

"Yes, um, it's really fine! It doesn't bother me at all. Ahaha..."

"...I see. Then let's call it settled at that. Thanks."

Daiki let out a breath of relief.

It had been a long time — in fact, it was the first time since he'd come back to Earth.

A thing that had never once come back to him even when he'd been fighting Oki: that ice down the spine.

Not murderous intent, but something past even that — the presence of death.

—**I thought I was going to be killed**.

He resolved not to forget it.

1-04 "The Former Hero, and the Witch" — 4
-------------------

Once the contract was over, Daiki rested for a little while and then set off home.

Oki urged him to stay the night at the «Hideout» (*safe area*), but Daiki firmly refused.

He had school the next day, and he couldn't think of an excuse for his family.

Above all — though he didn't say it out loud — being alone with Oki was unsettling.

"This place has a barrier, so it's safe, you know... it's easy to defend, too."

Oki let it drop in a crestfallen sort of way.

He didn't know the reason, but she had apparently wanted him to stay.

Even so, Oki didn't press hard. Instead she wouldn't budge on walking him home, and on that Daiki had no counterargument.

Walking a girl home was one thing, but being walked home by one was a sad story — still, he had to swallow it.

—On that walk home, Oki gave Daiki a warning.

"From now until this matter is settled, as a rule, don't be alone. It's fine for you to keep going to school, and after school you'll be acting together with me, but when I'm not there I want you in places where people can see you as much as possible. Most magi can't do a thing under those conditions."

Walking the night streets, emptier of people now, Oki spoke.

That they could walk like this was surely because she was with him.

"Huh... so that's how it is. They won't just attack you head-on."

"Of course not. If it turns into a police matter it's more trouble than you can imagine. In a sense it's more of a nuisance than making an enemy of another magus — if you get put on a wanted list you can't even pop out to a convenience store."

"Isn't that the kind of thing you can fix with magecraft...?"

Somehow it was all rather grubby, or something.

The value of the impressive phrase *a magus lurking quietly in a corner of the modern world* was collapsing rapidly.

"That's dreaming too much about magecraft. Or is it too much other world, rather? I can't say it's absolutely impossible, but fundamentally magi don't have anything like the abilities you're imagining."

"Huh... Among the mages I knew in the other world, the strongest was at the level of being able to smash an entire army from the front. So it's not like that, then."

"Could you not lump us in with something out of a manga?"

Oki said it as if exasperated.

Daiki thought that Oki was plenty manga-like (*fantasy*) herself.

"If you search, I think there are people like that too, in extremely small numbers. But you may as well treat that as the exception. They're about as rare as a tsuchinoko. Normally you can't do whatever you please to that degree."

"Well, if they could, it'd have made world news long ago."

"Whether they *can* and whether they *would* are separate questions, I'd say."

"Fair. I'm convinced."

—What if he'd come back to Earth still holding his combat abilities from the other world?

Daiki imagined it, and reached the conclusion that nothing much would be different from now.

He would obviously have hidden power like that. Standing out gained you nothing.

"That said, in exchange they tend to be skilled with underhanded methods. What counts as troublesome really depends on the time and the situation."

"That I understand. If my family were taken hostage, for instance... I'd have no choice but to comply."

In Daiki's experience there had been battles against enemies who armed themselves with intrigue.

Indeed, such things sometimes worked to greater effect than pure combat ability.

"I'm starting to get anxious."

"You're fine for now. I've got a certain amount of information on the enemy, but he's got nothing on us. Being chased by an opponent whose identity you don't know is pretty rough."

"Well, I follow that logic."

"And he doesn't seem to be a combat-oriented magus, either. Although a combat-oriented magus is rare to begin with. In the modern age, combat ability is basically garbage."

"...Garbage..."

"I mean, you don't need it, do you. It has far too few uses. You can use magecraft, and you spend it on fighting? That's a criminal waste of talent. At most you regard it as a by-product of your training in the magical arts — that's the theory among modern magi."

For Daiki, who had prided himself on his combat ability in the other world, those words hit somewhere tender.

Though to put it another way, it meant that Oki had displayed all that combat ability with a mere *by-product* of the arts she had mastered.

Which meant the present Daiki fell well short of even that *mere*.

"For all that talk, you've done plenty of hand-to-hand training, haven't you. You were strong."

"Self-defence is a girl's basic accomplishment."

"Self... defence...?"

That was not the level of it, he thought — Daiki made the appeal with a flat stare.

"It's not my fault if you look at me with that exasperated face. From where I stand, you're the far stranger one — putting up that much resistance bare-handed, without using any magical energy."

"Old skills, I guess. My body doesn't move the way I picture it at all anymore."

"Hmm...? I wonder how strong you were, back when you were in the other world."

Oki murmured it idly. Daiki shrugged lightly.

"...Who knows. In the end it was all founded on the holy sword — which is to say, on cheating. It's not as if I had any special talent myself, so it doesn't really register."

"Huh... Still, a holy sword. Can I ask what kind of sword it was?"

"What kind... hmm. It wasn't as flashy a sword as its name. Quiet type."

"I don't really know what a quiet sword means... is it a swordsman thing?"

"Hm? Ah, right. Actually, the sword I carried had a will of its own. It talked."

"—A talking sword?!"

"It was a good sort. Makes me nostalgic... I wonder what it's up to now. Stuck in something again, probably."

"No, you can't go from reminiscing about an old friend to 'stuck in something.'"

"It does feel rather like an old friend. It was a fairly sharp sort... in form."

"In form..."

"It was like a sword — no, it's fair to say it was a sword itself."

"Because it *is* a sword itself... huh, right? We are talking about a sword itself, aren't we...?"

—And so, in a fairly relaxed way, the two carried on chatting.

Unexpectedly — that was Daiki's impression — Oki was a decent listener.

She asked him all sorts of things about the other world, and Daiki spoke of it with fondness.

He truly did feel nostalgic.

Only a year had passed, and yet somehow it already felt like the distant past.

But his memory was vivid, and talking about it made it feel like yesterday.

That was surely because it was the first time he'd told anyone about the other world.

They were by no means only happy memories.

But nor were they things he had to hide.

And so.

Just a little, he began to regret that they would reach his house—

"—Ah. Round that corner and it's my place. Sorry for making you come so far."

Daiki spoke without letting a hint of the small feeling that had budded in him show.

Oki nodded.

"Mm. Nah, if anything it's nearby, so it was easy. Ah, I'll see you all the way inside, just in case."

"Thanks for the consideration... though I feel like the roles ought to be reversed."

"You care about that sort of thing, surprisingly. In that case, once this business is over, maybe I'll have you walk me home at the end?"

"If you really want that, I don't mind... ah, huh."

Turning the corner, Daiki's family home stood directly ahead.

And Daiki was startled to see a familiar figure standing in front of it.

"—Quite the late return, Daiki-senpai."

"Nagi..."

Saying his sister's name, Daiki rubbed the back of his neck awkwardly.

"Yes, it's Nagi. —And who might that be?"

Nagi approached the two of them briskly, with a graceful smile.

Oki said quietly,

"Huh, what? The girl-next-door kouhai childhood friend type?"

"Where did that come from."

Daiki answered under his breath as well.

"No... she's my little sister. Nominally. Nagi Kurosu."

"...Huh..."

"Ah, and keep the crack in my arm a secret. I don't want to worry her unnecessarily."

"—Are we telling secrets?"

Reaching them, Nagi stopped and turned a sulky, flat-eyed look on Daiki.

At times like these it was easier not to fumble around for excuses. Daiki shrugged.

"That's right, secrets. Ones I'm keeping from you."

"Mrrgh..."

"Right, thanks for walking me back. See you."

That was all he said; then, meaning to smoke-screen the rest, Daiki began pushing Nagi back toward the house.

Nagi turned a dissatisfied look on him, but perhaps unable to defy him, she was pushed along, dragging her feet.

—They really are close...

Oki thought. The eyes looking at her were blatantly suspicious.

And right in front of that magus—

"Come on, inside. And don't tell me you were waiting in front of the house."

"Of course not. I was simply enjoying the night air a little."

"What are you even saying..."

"You're the one who should explain, senpai. What is all this? I demand an explanation."

"Why are you sulking..."

"I will sulk. I went to the trouble of making dinner to celebrate your midterms and waiting for you. It's gone completely cold, though!"

"Sorry. Then I'll warm it up and eat it."

"There isn't any left for senpai anymore."

Carrying on that exchange, Daiki made eye contact with Oki.

—Well, being a big brother must have its hardships.

Being an only child, Oki understood it roughly like that, and saw the two of them off with a light wave.

"...Honestly. You're always making me worry."

"You're exaggerating; I'm a high-school student. And could you get over it already..."

"Hmph."

Nagi still seemed dissatisfied, but even so she opened the front door herself, then turned to face Daiki.

"Welcome home, nii-san."

"Mm. —I'm home."

And, respectfully, she welcomed her family in.

　　　　　※

And then.

Having watched Daiki go inside with his sister, Narumi Oki finally let out a breath.

—**It went well.**

There had been a moment when she'd genuinely wondered how it would turn out, but landing the contract counted as an excellent result.

Daiki didn't seem to have thought it through, but even if he came to regret it — it was already too late.

He hadn't understood in the slightest the weight of the act of «forming a contract» with a magus.

"Right... I'd better get this done quickly."

The night was long. Magi were creatures of the night to begin with.

For Oki, the hours of activity were only just starting.

—The girl's eyes stained a deep, blood-like crimson.

"..."

Still, it had been a surprise.

To think she'd run into a genuine other-world experiencer.

Once something like a *monster* had turned up, the possibility that his words were the truth couldn't be ignored.

And once she knew that, **there was absolutely no way Oki could let Daiki get away**.

She'd been willing to use somewhat forceful methods to bring him to a contract, but—

—**She hadn't imagined he'd resist the Mystic Eye, however imperfectly.**

Honestly, Oki had panicked rather badly at the time.

For some reason Daiki had accepted the story that she'd activated the Mystic Eye purely for atmosphere — but obviously that wasn't the case.

It was true that it activated of its own accord when her emotions ran high.

But when she proposed the contract, at least, Oki had deliberately switched the Mystic Eye on.

—And it hadn't worked on him.

No — more precisely, it would be closer to the truth to say that **although it should have been working, it did not seem to be producing any effect**.

Frankly, Oki had no idea what that meant.

Well — thanks to the follow-up afterward, the contract had been safely concluded. That was fine.

The rest was preparation.

Oki excited the magical energy held within her.

"..."

Still.

Talking with Daiki had been — a little bit fun.

Granted she'd had the aim of drawing out information about the other world; even so, it was a fact that he was enjoyable to talk to.

Even when the conversation wouldn't go the way she wanted, it felt somehow bashful.

She didn't really understand it, but it left her feeling oddly ticklish.

Perhaps because it had been a very long time since she'd talked with anyone like that—

"—No, no. I have to concentrate."

She shook her head and put her mind in order.

If a colleague in the trade saw her being this pathetic, she'd be a laughing stock.

She would see the job through as a professional.

"...Still, though."

The scene from a moment ago came back to her.

That girl — Nagi, was it? She'd been regarded with a great deal of suspicion.

Honestly, what a close pair of siblings. Close enough that one might mistake them for lovers of many years' standing.

That much — how to put it — there had been real affection in that girl's eyes.

So it wouldn't be unreasonable to make the mistake.

After all,

"**They didn't look like siblings**, did they... heh."

The brother was unremarkable enough, granted.

But the sister had **long white hair** and **golden eyes**. Of course one would jump to conclusions.

"...Maybe they're not actually related by blood. Well... that's hard to ask about, I suppose."

Turning such things over in her mind—

—the magus's night deepened further.

1-05 "The Former Hero's Everyday Life" — 1
-------------------

The night passed.

The next day, Daiki Kurosu went to school as usual, naturally.

If one insisted on calling the return from the extraordinary to the ordinary a rehabilitation, then Daiki could be said to have spent a year at it, and his body was only now beginning to grow used to it.

He didn't want to break that pace. That was Daiki's honest feeling.

Adapting to the extraordinary that was another world might, in hindsight, have been the easier task—

Looking back, Daiki thought so.

He couldn't claim with a straight face that his mental state had been stable when he was first thrown into the other world, but when it was a matter of life and death, one couldn't spend all day complaining.

The result was that he adapted to life in the other world by being cornered into it.

It hadn't been a situation in which he could say he wasn't used to it. Once he'd been dropped in, there was no method available but to manage.

Even so.

Daiki had never imagined he would struggle with the reverse.

"Hmmmm..."

Seeing Daiki brooding, his friend tilted his head and asked,

"What's up, Daiki? It's rare for you to get like that."

"Hm? Ah, no, it's nothing major... and it isn't that rare, is it. Until just recently I was agonising plenty over midterm prep."

Daiki said it with a wry smile.

Whether it belonged under the heading of adapting to ordinary life was debatable, but in a sense the greatest crisis the returned Daiki had faced was — to hide nothing — his grades. Which is to say, study.

He remembered none of it.

He couldn't keep up at all—

The school being an academically ambitious one, recovering the study knowledge lost during his long life in the other world could fairly be called the problem that tormented him most.

Even now he was only at the level of having maybe, possibly, clawed back to average.

—Thank God he'd been transferred in the spring of his first year.

Daiki was fairly seriously grateful for that. If it had been right before entrance exams it would have been unwatchable.

"Well, sure. But you don't really strike me as the type to brood much, Daiki."

His friend said it after Daiki brought up studying.

"Don't I?"

"Nah. You're kind of grown up, or something."

"————"

They were surely idle words, but Daiki froze for a moment.

After all, in the other world Daiki had grown to roughly the age of twenty. Coming back to Earth had rolled his growth back — or rather returned him to a self who hadn't grown yet — but in any case, in terms of mental age, he was about half a generation ahead of the classmates around him.

He didn't consider the gap large enough to be worth minding.
